Distortion and lo-fi production act as a ghostly mask for what are, in many ways, quite joyful reinterpretations of British folk sounds. Their folkloric trip hop takes existing historical and cultural source material – such as a mid-twentieth-century folklore journal, a book on Welsh myths, and another on Bronze-Age human remains – and drags it into the modern era. Their latest release, Remscéla (2025), is a partial reimagining of Irish poet Thomas Kinsella's canonical translation of the Irish pagan epic, Táin Bó Cúailnge – the tale of an invader queen who seeks to plunder a magical bull from her former husband. In the same way that these remscéla act as a preface in Kinsella's translation, Milkweed's album of the same title is a beautiful, curious introduction to what one can expect at one of their live shows: two mysterious people heartedly keeping oral storytelling traditions alive through wispy song and mischievous experimentation.\n\n**LIO**\n\nFormed in summer of 1998, the London Improvisers Orchestra has been dedicated to large ensemble conducted improvisation, or ‘Conduction’, and free improvisation, for over 25 years. New York City Jazz Record described the LIO as \"one of the essential institutions of improvised music\".\n\nThe orchestra rose from the ashes of the 1997 touring group of American improviser Butch Morris, the pioneering inventor of Conduction. Since its birth, the group has had hundreds of members pass through its ranks.\n\nFor more information on access at EartH, please see our FAQs here: https://bit.ly/44dzl75 or contact us at access@earthackney.co.uk\n\nEartH is a social enterprise committed to supporting new musicians, with music studios in the venue that are completely free for young artists to use. Their folkloric trip hop takes existing historical and cultural source material – such as a mid-twentieth-century folklore journal, a book on Welsh myths, and another on Bronze-Age human remains – and drags it into the modern era. Their latest release, Remscéla (2025), is a partial reimagining of Irish poet Thomas Kinsella's canonical translation of the Irish pagan epic, Táin Bó Cúailnge – the tale of an invader queen who seeks to plunder a magical bull from her former husband. In the same way that these remscéla act as a preface in Kinsella's translation, Milkweed's album of the same title is a beautiful, curious introduction to what one can expect at one of their live shows: two mysterious people heartedly keeping oral storytelling traditions alive through wispy song and mischievous experimentation.\n\n**LIO**\n\nFormed in summer of 1998, the London Improvisers Orchestra has been dedicated to large ensemble conducted improvisation, or ‘Conduction’, and free improvisation, for over 25 years. New York City Jazz Record described the LIO as \"one of the essential institutions of improvised music\".\n\nThe orchestra rose from the ashes of the 1997 touring group of American improviser Butch Morris, the pioneering inventor of Conduction. For accessibility tickets please purchase directly from the venue.\n\nLips Choir\n\nFormed in 2009, Lips Choir has grown from a small group of friends who loved to sing, to a 100-strong musical force to be reckoned with. Led by dynamic musical director Jessie Maryon Davies, Lips were handpicked by Alison Goldfrapp to perform alongside her at the Royal Albert Hall, where Goldfrapp described them as “Great singers with a wonderful musical sensibility.\". Other highlights of the last 17 years include performances at Mighty Hoopla, Bestival, Camp Bestival and Wilderness Festival plus sell-out shows at Earth Hackney, the Coronet and Clapham Grand. Last year, Lips was proud to perform the world premiere of REIGN by Errollyn Wallen for WOW15 at the Royal Albert Hall in an intergenerational choir of over 100 singers. Lips is a women’s choir for people who identify as female, trans and gender non-conforming.\n\nYvette Riby-Williams (NAALA)\n\nYvette Riby-Williams is a London-based Lecturer, singer, songwriter, producer and workshop Lead working in communities across the UK.\n\nShe has sung with many prominent musicians including Jarvis Cocker, Bobby McFerrin, Nao, Heidi Vogel and the Cinematic Orchestra. As a songwriter she has co-written with Daktyl for his album Unreality, Mercury nominated Kathryn Williams’ album Night Drives, NAO for her Grammy nominated album “Saturn” and collaborated with Jarvis Cocker on his album and live cave performance of “Beyond the Pale”, being heavily featured on the remix of 'House Music All Night Long', Touring with Pulp this Summer for their Mercury nominated release ‘More’. Much like her musical tastes she draws inspiration from everywhere; in the sounds of vivid horn lines of vintage Jazz records to the hypnotic grooves of Ghanaian highlife cuts to weave these into her uniquely magical storytelling.\n\nDJ Eula\n\nEula (she/her, fka SHANDY)! Eula is founder of GAYTORADE, a retired bagpiping prodigy and London-based, Scottish, transexual anarchist river dyke. Formed in Belgium in the late 2000s, the band quickly became a vital force in the international underground scene, known for their unflinching intensity, raw vulnerability, and transformative live performances.\n\nFollowing a hiatus that began in 2018, the band is returning to the stage in 2026, starting with a highly anticipated performance at Roadburn — where they will play Rheia in its entirety, ten years after its release. Though the members remained creatively active during Oathbreaker’s dormancy (through projects such as Wiegedood, Siem Reap and Predatory Void), the spirit and weight of Oathbreaker’s music never truly went silent.\n\nMore than just a reunion, Oathbreaker’s re-emergence reaffirms the urgency, relevance, and emotional depth that have always set them apart. Their legacy is not only intact — it continues to grow.\n\nIf you have any queries or would like more information about access at Village Underground, please see our FAQs here: https://bit.ly/3EhSLgF, or contact access@villageunderground.co.uk.\n\nPresented by Old Empire & Born Again Concerts. \n\nThis is a 16+ event. It’s all in a name, sometimes. The danceable, costumed, curiosity rich duo of artists Freya Tate and Moina Moin are as imaginative as they profess. Or, to get more to the point, as we all need them to be.\n\nFreya and Moina are two visual artists and musicians from Stroud and South London, places where they importantly found communities (Stroud’s SVA and the capital’s Total Refreshment Centre) of like minded people just as willing to chase down an idea to its possibly illogical conclusion. And it is in the collective and the idea of participation that Mermaid Chunky really clicks. This is a party, a collective dance, made all the better with more: people, ideas, layers, kick drums, recorders, saxophones, frogs.\n\nTo wit, the album’s first track and first single, “Céilí,” named after a traditional Scottish or Irish social gathering and dance, which builds from a simple recorder line into a swelling, warm burst of major chord dance music. Goosebumps or check your pulse.\n\nFurther down the rabbit hole, “Chaperone” is almost boardwalk electro, like Fischerspooner on a ferris wheel; “Frogsporn” and “Nature Girl” are mucky, trippy dirges filled with stalactites of synth and squelch; “Tiny Gymnast” is a kaleidoscopic waltz into the night. Hold onto your seats, ladies and gentlemen.\n\n\\----\n\nTaliaBle is a Tottenham raised rapper and multidisciplinary artist, prized as thee go to underground circus. Since emerging in 2019, she has sculpted a distinct lane blending tongue twisting, poetic lyricism with experimental electronic and left field hip hop beats. Her self-directed visual world folds magic into everyday London concrete. You’ll experience a world full of surplus whimsy, grit, and raw stories, as her music moves between chaos and clarity.\n\nGaining the attention from i.D magazine, The Face, Fred Perry Subculture and Charles Jeffrey paired with a headline gig at Tate Britain; TaliaBle has been hailed as ‘exciting, experimental and punk as hell.’\n\nRevered as a powerhouse of sound and image, she crafts immersive live experiences that blur the line between performance and visual spectacle. Expect thought provoking, high energy shows filled with distortion and moments of collective catharsis.\n\nFrom legendary stages like the 100 Club, Roundhouse, KOKO, and Paradiso Amsterdam, to cultural institutions such as Tate Britain and the ICA, and festivals like OutBreak, Horst, and Green Man; TaliaBle commands every room she steps into. It weaves issues of faith, belonging, and anxiety into a modern folk record that feels increasingly more monumental by the minute. Much like the artists who inspire him—like Sufjan Stevens, Pedro the Lion, and Weed (pre-Hotline TNT)—Hudson Freeman finds himself at a uniquely turbulent coming-of-age moment. His music is shaped by a religious upbringing, grappling with moral absolutism, fleeting friendships, and navigating a search for belonging online.\n\nThen, the riff took the world by storm. His new song \"If You Know Me\" drew in attention from the masses, catapulting Freeman into a wider spotlight than ever. It's a moody blend of bedroom pop, folk, slowcore, and post-emo, stitched together by a singular guitar riff and mantra-style lyrics, confirming Hudson's undeniable knack for melody. The demo turned barnburner “If You Know Me” sent Freeman into the spotlight, released last November with a blown out cover of The Stones'/Flying Burrito Brothers' “Wild Horses” to boot.\n\nEarlier this year, he released a split EP, Adult Contemporary, with his friend The Bedroomer—a collaborative effort seeing the duo dive deeper into similar topics and sounds as Freeman continues to hone his sound and explore life’s curiosities.\n\nNow, nearly a year after \"If You Know Me\" changed Freeman’s life, he’s working harder than ever. Spending more time than ever on the road, 2026 has seen Hudson take the stage at Luck Reunion, Newport Folk Fest, opening slots for Mumford & Sons and Foxwarren, plus sold-out headline shows across the country and across the pond. At a party, Helena Deland slips toward the edge of the room; no one sees her as she eyes a notebook which belongs to the party’s host. She insists she would never read it, then lets herself imagine being caught, only to learn that its owner had wanted to be discovered all along. The journal becomes a mirror of songwriting, private experience made public and placed in strangers’ hands.\n\nDeland’s third solo album follows the electronic density of Someone New (2020) and the grief-shadowed folk of Goodnight Summerland (2023). Recorded in rural Quebec in bursts over four seasons, Eternity Shrugs was produced by Deland with longtime bandmates Francis Ledoux and Alexandre Larin, as well as musicians Lysandre Ménard and Cedric Martel who have played with the band for years. Piano, bass, organ, Rhodes, viola, and woodwinds gather around her voice and guitar in patient arrangements anchored by finely textured percussion; the songs can feel spare one moment and subtly ornate the next.\n\nNear the record’s centre, the piano-lit “Room” blooms with an external voice addressing Deland in the second person, a returning motif that allows one of the album’s characters to speak back to its author. In “Great Dreams,” a meteor shower and an easy silence widen into cinema through 1960s-era strings and a bass line that glances toward Lee Hazlewood, while Montreal contemporary Cindy Lee flickers at the edge of the frame. Deland imagines the album’s palette in browns: wood, leather, smoke. Warm and inhabited, the music never entirely settles.\n\n“Where I Stand,” inspired by seeing Lucinda Williams perform at the Winnipeg Folk Festival, turns the infinite into a missed call: eternity spins Deland across an empty dance floor, then shrugs and hangs up. The closing “Here on the Page” returns to the furtive notebook, asking what becomes of experience if it is not written down, though with its slow and dirge-like gravity, suggests that even words committed to paper remain vulnerable to time. Deland leaves these private traces in public anyway, hoping to hold what is passing and to see who might meet her there.\n\nFor more information on access at EartH, please see our FAQs here: https://bit.ly/44dzl75 or contact us at access@earthackney.co.uk\n\nEartH is a social enterprise committed to supporting new musicians, with music studios in the venue that are completely free for young artists to use. Regan","time":""}],"links":[],"price":null,"type_tags":["music:gig"],"sold_out":false},{"show_price_breakdown":false,"images":["https://dice-media.imgix.net/attachments/2026-11-24/0d3fcf3a-a675-4e53-bba5-bdc01c2a200c.jpg"],"external_url":null,"venue":"EartH","sale_end_date":"2027-03-23T21:00:00Z","destination_event_perm_name":null,"sale_start_date":"2026-11-27T10:00:00Z","type":"event","featured":false,"cities":[{"code":"london","country_alpha3":"GBR","country_name":"United Kingdom","id":"54d8a23438fe5d27d500001c","name":"London"}],"perm_name":"exre-23rd-mar-earth-london-tickets","announcement_date":"2026-11-24T12:00:00Z","is_multi_days_event":false,"checksum":"01A5C7F56C1ABD704005B76A055E4971","hash":"bvloxo","raw_description":"This event will take place in EartH Theatre.\n\nElena Tonra didn’t intend to write a second Ex:Re album. The lead singer of Daughter released a solo record in 2018 and had only ever conceived of one record proper under that moniker. But in 2023, when she took some sketches of songs she’d had for several years into the studio with Fabian Prynn, who produced the first Ex:Re album, the pair’s musical compatibility once again took hold, and Save As was born.\n\nThe title of album opener ‘Radio Station’, which begins with stately strummed acoustic guitar and morphs into a trippy song about an endless California road trip, is a metaphor for how Tonra feels about her mind. “The changing radio station is something I experience a lot – the push and pull of different feelings, and I end up getting stuck because I can’t make decisions,” she says. The song is formed from snippets of Tonra’s memories overlaid with scraps of overheard conversations. “I’ve ordered your coffee/Let’s fry all our money,” she sings, over a heady wall of guitars and Prynn’s upfront percussion. \n\nThere is no grand, overarching storyline to Save As. Rather, the album is the latest in Tonra’s catalogue that expertly chronicles the gnawing minutiae of human consciousness: the over-thinking that consumes us, and the places we travel to escape. Its central theme is how we remember these moments. “My memory is not always very good,” Tonra admits. By preserving images in songs, she gives herself a means to remember. Listening to or performing them “is like travelling back in time”.\n\nFor example, Tonra remembers exactly where she was when she wrote ‘Sleep’ – and just how she was feeling. She was alone in Paris, where she recorded a demo in her hotel, a texture that lives on in the track’s final cut, which features a melodic loop made from the demo’s acoustic guitar run through a vocoder and a drum machine. There’s a muffled texture to that sound, like hearing a party through a wall. Tonra’s highly felt alt-pop is not the first genre you’d expect to hear on the dance floor, but ‘Sleep’ is “something that I’d dance to!”, she says. Its vibe is “late night, after hours” – and defies the song’s title. That demo recordings live on in many of the final cuts is key to the sentiment of Save As: this is where we got to; we could record more and more versions; but preserving what we’ve got now is what would be most true. \n\nOther songs found their true version in the studio, after improvisation sessions that Tonra and Prynn always recorded, just in case. On the stand-out ‘Am I Even Real?’, a Tonra-Prynn duet that was built out from its first ever recording, Tonra sings about spaceships to ask whether she is being true to herself in a relationship. It began as a “really slow, really sad song”, she recalls. “Then one day we thought, ‘What would happen if we just…’ and we started playing the chorus really fast.” It stuck. That combination of long-held ideas stored in notebooks and on laptops with unfiltered first thoughts and improvisations is key to the album’s perfect mingling of liveliness and deep thoughtfulness.\n\nThe record’s second half brings a more pared-back sonic texture, and with it some of the most direct songwriting of Tonra’s career. On ‘Frozen Aisle’, she sings of browsing for ice cream in the supermarket when she is overcome by a crushing feeling, a sensation far too overwhelming for the mundane situation she is in. How to create the sound of trying not to burst into tears in a public place? Tonra asked the assembled band – which included her long-time collaborator Josephine Stephenson on cello, Prynn on harmonium, Hinako Omori on synthesisers, Ben Reed on bass and Jethro Fox on electric guitar – if they would try to make their instruments sound like they were melting. “No good things to say” is Tonra’s refrain as the band bends its sound, disintegrating around her. Such direct lyricism is also wonderfully apparent on ‘Wasp’, on which Tonra sings of catching a creature in a jar and releasing it out the back door.\n\nFor all this straight-talking, you can still rely on Tonra to provide some of the most elegant lyrics in modern songwriting. “Sometimes a death just has no deeper meaning / Than time,” she sings on ‘Healing (Slow)’, the album’s final track, which is about grieving the death of her grandmother. It’s a hopeful, loving track that brings the Ex:Re project back to its component parts – Tonra on vocals and guitars; Prynn on drums – and holds the human warmth of this most special of musical collaborations at its very core. \n\nFor more information on access at EartH, please see our FAQs here: https://bit.ly/44dzl75 or contact us at access@earthackney.co.uk\n\nEartH is a social enterprise committed to supporting new musicians, with music studios in the venue that are completely free for young artists to use. When you attend a show in EartH Theatre, head to the back of the space to rent a cushion for just £2. Not only will you get to sit more comfortably, but all proceeds go towards funding Studio 36.
